    50→\begin{abstract}
    51→Adapting pretrained time series foundation models (TSFMs) to downstream tasks currently relies on static, single-head adapters. While dynamically routing across diverse adapter topologies via mixture-of-experts (MoE) offers a path to task-specific specialization, we identify a fatal structural bottleneck in temporal adaptation: \textbf{gradient co-adaptation}. We demonstrate that standard MoE routing on TSFM hidden states collapses to a single expert---routing entropy dropping to $0.000\pm0.000$ (3 seeds)---particularly when backbone layers are unfrozen, as deep representations co-adapt to reinforce the dominant expert.
    52→To break this collapse, we propose \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}. RR-MoA bypasses the backbone's internal Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin}) entirely, utilizing a lightweight gating network that reads the raw, \emph{pre-RevIN} input signal to preserve the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that the backbone would otherwise strip away before routing. A direct ablation shows that applying per-window RevIN to the router's input degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ across all three datasets, empirically isolating rawness from bypass as the source of the gains. Crucially, we uncover a \textbf{``Frozen Paradox''}: keeping the backbone strictly frozen prevents co-adaptation and yields \emph{superior} MoE routing compared to standard partial fine-tuning. Evaluated rigorously across three seeds, strictly frozen RR-MoA achieves \textbf{27/27 wins} against single-adapter baselines and \textbf{9/9 wins} against a modern PEFT baseline (LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}) under the identical protocol, yielding a $43.5\%$ MSE reduction on ETTh1 ($0.690{\pm}0.02$), $51.1\%$ on ETTm1 ($0.572{\pm}0.07$), and $44.6\%$ on Weather ($0.289{\pm}0.01$). To populate the expert pool we further introduce \textbf{Adapter Architecture Search (AAS)}: an LLM-guided code-level search that imports cross-domain motifs (depthwise convolutions, BatchNorm gating, feature attention) into the TSFM adapter vocabulary. Finally, enforcing \textbf{Top-2 sparse routing} bounds the active expert FLOPs to $40\%$ of a dense mixture while preserving the MSE gains, establishing a compute-fair, Pareto-optimal paradigm for parameter-efficient TSFM adaptation.
    53→\end{abstract}
    54→
    55→\section{Introduction}
    56→\label{sec:intro}
    57→
    58→The transformer architecture~\citep{vaswani2017attention} has enabled foundation models~\citep{bommasani2021foundation} across language~\citep{devlin2019bert, brown2020gpt3} and vision~\citep{dosovitskiy2021vit}. Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer/Timer-XL~\citep{liu2024timer, liu2025timerxl}, Moirai/Moirai-MoE~\citep{woo2024moirai, liu2025moiraimoe}, UniTS~\citep{lee2024units}, Lag-Llama~\citep{rasul2024lagllama}, Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe}, and TTM~\citep{ekambaram2024ttm} have emerged as powerful pretrained representations for temporal tasks. Once a TSFM is pretrained, a concrete practical question determines whether it is useful in production: \textit{how should the adapter that connects it to a downstream task be designed and trained?}
    59→
    60→\textbf{The one-size-fits-all adapter status quo.} Current TSFM adaptation almost always reduces to the same computational template: \emph{pool the backbone's hidden states $\to$ project to the output horizon}, with a handful of standard variants---linear probing~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm}, MLP heads~\citep{nie2023patchtst, wu2023timesnet}, LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, and bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}. Crucially, every public TSFM benchmark we are aware of uses a \emph{single static} adapter for an entire dataset. Yet real time series are highly non-stationary: a dataset such as ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions, and it is far from obvious that a single adapter topology is optimal for every window. This observation motivates a \emph{mixture of adapters}: maintain a small pool of topologically distinct expert heads and let a learned router assign each window to the most appropriate expert per sample.
    61→
    62→\textbf{Why frozen at all?} Lightweight supervised baselines (DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear}) achieve materially lower absolute MSE than any frozen-backbone adapter on the same evaluation scale. Our contribution is not to replace single-dataset specialists but to optimize routing within the frozen-backbone paradigm motivated by (i)~multi-tenant cloud serving with hot-swappable per-tenant adapters, (ii)~on-device deployment where per-site retraining is infeasible, and (iii)~cross-task reuse where one backbone drives forecasting and imputation via different expert pools (Appendix~\ref{app:deployment}). As TSFMs scale toward billion-parameter regimes~\citep{shi2024timemoe}, frozen-backbone PEFT becomes the only viable per-dataset adaptation path.
    63→
    64→\textbf{The natural idea that fails: MoE on TSFMs.} When we apply the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---to a TSFM, it \emph{collapses catastrophically}. Across 3 seeds, 3 datasets, and every setting where backbone layers are unfrozen, routing entropy drops to exactly $0.000\pm0.000$ (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}): all weight concentrates on a single expert.
    65→
    66→\textbf{Diagnosis: gradient co-adaptation.} The collapse has a specific causal structure: whichever expert the router marginally prefers receives larger gradients; those flow back into the unfrozen backbone, which reshapes $\mathbf{H}$ to serve that expert better, completing a positive feedback loop within ${\sim}50$ steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). Freezing $\theta$ breaks this loop (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}: entropy recovers to $0.5$--$0.6$), but hidden-state routing still underperforms because the backbone's internal RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that would distinguish samples. A controlled ablation (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}) confirms this: applying RevIN to the router's input degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$. Unlike NLP/vision MoEs where tokens carry discrete identities surviving LayerNorm, time-series routing requires the macro-statistics that normalization strips.
    67→
    68→\textbf{Our fix.} We propose \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}: (i)~the router reads the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ (preserving per-window trend, amplitude, volatility), and (ii)~the backbone is kept strictly frozen ($\texttt{requires\_grad}{=}\texttt{False}$ for all $\theta$). Only the router and experts are trainable, reducing the optimization from $\theta_\text{active}\cup\phi\cup\psi$ to $\phi\cup\psi$ alone.
    69→
    70→\textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} In our 3\,datasets\,$\times$\,3\,freeze levels\,$\times$\,3\,seeds ablation (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}), strictly frozen RR-MoA is at least as good as partially unfrozen RR-MoA on every dataset, with $43.5\%$ and $51.1\%$ MSE reductions on ETTh1 and ETTm1. Unfreezing buys routing collapse, not expressivity---reversing the standard NLP PEFT heuristic.
    71→
    72→\textbf{Populating the expert pool.} For RR-MoA to work, the expert pool must be diverse. We formalize \textbf{Adapter Architecture Search (AAS)} as an LLM-guided code-level search over arbitrary PyTorch \texttt{nn.Module} programs that \emph{recovers} cross-domain motifs---depthwise convolutions~\citep{howard2017mobilenets}, BatchNorm~\citep{he2016resnet, ioffe2015batchnorm}, feature attention~\citep{hu2018senet}---into the TSFM adapter vocabulary. We distill the search logs into five canonical expert topologies used in all RR-MoA experiments.
    73→
    74→Our contributions: \textbf{(1)}~We diagnose \emph{gradient co-adaptation} as the cause of MoE routing collapse on TSFMs: hidden-state routing entropy drops to $0.000\pm0.000$ whenever backbone layers are unfrozen (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). \textbf{(2)}~We propose \textbf{RR-MoA}, which routes on the raw pre-normalization signal and keeps the backbone frozen, achieving \textbf{54/54 wins} across 6 datasets, 3 freeze levels, and 3 seeds (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}). \textbf{(3)}~We uncover the \textbf{Frozen Paradox}: strictly frozen routing outperforms partially unfrozen routing, reversing the standard PEFT heuristic. \textbf{(4)}~Top-2 sparse routing bounds active FLOPs to $40\%$ of a dense mixture (\S\ref{sec:topk}). \textbf{(5)}~\textbf{AAS} populates the expert pool via LLM-guided code-level search over an unbounded architecture space (\S\ref{sec:search_space}).
    75→
    76→\textbf{Gaps.} Recent NeurIPS 2025 work searches over finetuning \textit{strategies} (MSFT~\citep{qiao2025msft}), \textit{pruning} (Prune-then-Finetune~\citep{zhao2025prune}), or \textit{model selection} (TEMPLATE~\citep{zhang2025template})---but none searches the adapter \textit{architecture}. In the LLM-guided search community, PartEvo~\citep{hu2025partevo} and AI Research Agents~\citep{toledo2025aiagents} demonstrate code-level search for automated ML, but not for FM adaptation. We bridge these lines of work.
    77→
    78→\section{Related Work}
    79→
    80→\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} Standard adaptation uses LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} or bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters} with manually chosen configurations. MSFT~\citep{qiao2025msft} proposes multi-scale finetuning for encoder-based TSFMs. TRACE~\citep{li2025trace} introduces importance-based LoRA selection with reconstructed heads---the closest work, but heads are hand-designed. Beyond LoRA~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora} surveys PEFT alternatives for Chronos. AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} adapts input representations. Prune-then-Finetune~\citep{zhao2025prune} applies structured pruning. In-context fine-tuning~\citep{faw2025icf} adapts at inference time. TEMPLATE~\citep{zhang2025template} addresses model selection; GIFT-Eval~\citep{woo2024gifteval} provides benchmarking. All search over finetuning \textit{strategies}, not adapter \textit{architecture}---the gap we address.
    81→
    82→\textbf{NAS for Adapters.} NAS~\citep{zoph2017nas} has evolved through ENAS~\citep{pham2018enas}, DARTS~\citep{liu2019darts}, and regularized evolution~\citep{real2019regularized}. Shears~\citep{munoz2024shears} and RZ-NAS~\citep{ji2025rznas} search adapter hyperparameters for LLMs. NAS-LoRA~\citep{chen2025naslora} integrates NAS for vision adapters. ONE-NAS~\citep{li2022onenas} and TS-NAS~\citep{liang2024tsnas} search raw time series architectures but not adapters on pretrained backbones. No prior work searches an \textit{unbounded} architecture space for TSFM adapters.
    83→
    84→\textbf{LLM-Guided Code Search.} LLMs trained on code~\citep{chen2021codex} enable program-level search. FunSearch~\citep{romera2024funsearch}, ELM~\citep{lehman2023elm}, EvoPrompting~\citep{chen2023evoprompting}, ReEvo~\citep{ye2024reevo}, LLMatic~\citep{nasir2024llmatic}, PartEvo~\citep{hu2025partevo}, SOAR~\citep{pourcel2025soar}, EvoTune~\citep{surina2025evotune}, GENESYS~\citep{cheng2025genesys}, SEA-TS~\citep{xu2026seats}, and AI Research Agents~\citep{toledo2025aiagents} demonstrate evolutionary program synthesis across domains~\citep{sobania2023program, zhang2025llm4opt}. We apply this paradigm to TSFM adapter search---$1000\times$ cheaper than full-model evolution.
    85→
    86→\section{Adapter Architecture Search}
    87→\label{sec:aas}
    88→

   156→\caption{\textbf{RR-MoA architecture.} The raw input splits into two streams. \textit{Path~A}: the \emph{strictly frozen} TSFM produces hidden states $\mathbf{H}$ with no gradient flow through $\theta$. \textit{Path~B}: a lightweight gating network reads the raw pre-normalization signal and selects $k$ of $K$ experts via $\mathrm{TopK}$. Standard MoE (AdaMix) that routes on $\mathbf{H}$ collapses to a single expert with routing entropy exactly $0.000\pm0.000$ across 3 seeds whenever any backbone layer is unfrozen (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}); RR-MoA achieves \textbf{27/27 wins} across 3 datasets $\times$ 3 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}). The router's reliance on the \emph{content} of the raw signal---not merely the fact of bypass---is empirically isolated in Table~\ref{tab:router_input}: applying per-window RevIN to $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ before the gate degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$.}

   159→
   160→\subsection{Problem Formulation}
   161→
   162→An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states to forecasts, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. An \emph{architecture space} $\mathcal{G}$ is a set of adapter specifications, each defining a computational graph, parameter space, and initialization (formal definitions in Appendix~\ref{app:formal_defs}).
   163→
   164→\begin{definition}[Adapter Architecture Search]
   165→\label{def:aas}
   166→Given backbone $f_\theta$, architecture space $\mathcal{G}$, and dataset $\mathcal{D} = \mathcal{D}_\text{train} \cup \mathcal{D}_\text{val} \cup \mathcal{D}_\text{test}$, the \textbf{AAS problem} is the bilevel optimization:
   167→\begin{equation}
   168→g^* = \arg\min_{g \in \mathcal{G}} \; \mathcal{L}\!\left(g_{\hat{\phi}} \circ f_{\hat{\theta}_\text{act}}, \; \mathcal{D}_\text{val}\right), \quad \text{where} \quad \{\hat{\phi}, \hat{\theta}_\text{act}\} = \arg\min_{\phi, \theta_\text{act}} \; \mathcal{L}\!\left(g_\phi \circ f_{\theta}, \; \mathcal{D}_\text{train}\right)
   169→\label{eq:aas}
   170→\end{equation}
   171→\end{definition}
   172→
   173→This separates two decisions conflated in current TSFM practice: \textit{what computational graph} the adapter should implement (outer loop) and \textit{what weights} that graph should have (inner loop).
   174→
   175→\subsection{Search Space Hierarchy}
   176→\label{sec:search_space}
   177→
   178→We define three nested spaces: a \emph{discrete} space $\mathcal{C}$ (${\sim}2{,}160$ configurations from Cartesian products of adapter hyperparameters), a \emph{template} space $\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$ (${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures from a context-free grammar; Appendix~\ref{app:grammar}), and a \emph{code} space $\mathcal{G}_\text{code}$ (all valid PyTorch \texttt{nn.Module} programs under $500$K params). These form a strict hierarchy $\mathcal{C}_\text{head} \subset \mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl} \subset \mathcal{G}_\text{code}$ (proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}; expressiveness table in Appendix~\ref{app:expressiveness}). The winning AAS architectures---depthwise convolutions, BatchNorm, feature attention---lie in $\mathcal{G}_\text{code} \setminus \mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$.
   179→
   180→\subsection{Search Instantiations}
   181→
   182→\textbf{Population and search budget.} We maintain a population of 20 adapters evolved over 12 generations (${\sim}240$ total evaluations), comparable in scale to EvoPrompting~\citep{chen2023evoprompting} (${\sim}200$). Each evaluation performs a short fitness probe (3 training epochs on the validation split) on a single A10G GPU. Elitism preserves the top 2 architectures each generation. The population is initialized with 5 hand-designed seed adapters plus 15 randomly generated ones from the template grammar.
   183→
   184→\textbf{Template-based code generator.} The default search in AAS samples from $\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$ (Definition~\ref{def:template}), inspired by grammar-guided program synthesis~\citep{koza1994genetic, mckay2010grammar}. The generator constructs complete \texttt{\_\_init\_\_} and \texttt{forward} methods as code strings by composing a pooling strategy, MLP, and output layer, with shape correctness guaranteed by construction. This yields 100\% validity (vs.\ ${\sim}75\%$ for LLM-generated code in $\mathcal{G}_\text{code}$), enabling fair comparison that isolates search space effects from evaluation budget differences.
   185→
   186→\textbf{Evolutionary loop.} Each generation: (1)~validate candidates locally, (2)~evaluate valid adapters on GPU (3 epochs, fitness $= -\text{MSE}_\text{val}$), (3)~rank and preserve top 2 via elitism, (4)~fill 18 slots with new template samples. After 12 generations, top-5 are re-evaluated at 15 epochs on the held-out test set. An LLM variant (gpt-4o-mini) generates adapters in $\mathcal{G}_\text{code}$ using evolutionary context.
   187→
   188→\textbf{Standard protocol.} Chronological train/val/test splits per the LTSF benchmark~\citep{zhou2021informer}: ETT-hourly (8640/2880/2880), ETT-minutely (34560/11520/11520), Weather/Electricity (60/20/20\%). Architecture search uses the validation set; final evaluation uses the test set.
   189→
   190→\subsection{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
   191→\label{sec:rrmoa}
   192→
   193→AAS discovers topologically diverse adapters, but deploying them individually requires per-dataset search. A natural alternative is MoE routing, but standard hidden-state routing \emph{collapses catastrophically} on TSFMs due to gradient co-adaptation (\S\ref{sec:intro}). We propose \textbf{RR-MoA}, which splits the information stream: the TSFM processes $\mathbf{H}$, while a lightweight gate reads the \textit{raw, pre-normalization} input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ to compute sparse Top-$k$ routing weights:
   194→\begin{equation}
   195→\mathbf{Y} = \sum_{j \in \mathcal{T}} \underbrace{\widetilde{w}_j}_{\text{sparse weight}} \cdot \underbrace{\text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H}_\text{FM})}_{\text{deep embedding branch}},\quad
   196→\mathcal{T} = \mathrm{TopK}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}),\, k\right),\;\;
   197→\widetilde{w} = \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})\right)
   198→\label{eq:rrmoa}
   199→\end{equation}
   200→where $G_\psi$ is a small Conv1d\,+\,pooling\,+\,linear gate ($\sim$1.1K params) operating directly on the raw time series, and each $\text{Expert}_k$ is an AAS-distilled adapter head. Routing is restricted to the Top-$k$ experts with the highest gate logits, so only $k$ of $K$ experts execute per sample. With $K{=}5$, $k{=}2$ we obtain $40\%$ of the dense FLOPs while retaining $>\!94\%$ of the MSE gain (\S\ref{sec:topk}). The raw input preserves the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that RevIN strips away, enabling per-sample routing that reflects the physical characteristics of each time series window.
   201→
   202→\begin{algorithm}[h]
   203→\caption{RR-MoA: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters with Top-$k$ sparse routing}
   204→\label{alg:rrmoa}
   205→\begin{algorithmic}[1]
   206→\REQUIRE Strictly frozen TSFM $f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}$, raw input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times L}$, $K$ expert adapters, sparsity $k\leq K$
   207→\STATE $\mathbf{H}\leftarrow f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Deep embeddings; \textbf{no gradients} through $\theta$}
   208→\STATE $\mathbf{g}\leftarrow G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Raw-signal gate logits, $\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times K}$}
   209→\STATE $\mathcal{T}\leftarrow \mathrm{TopK}(\mathbf{g},\,k)$ \hfill \COMMENT{Per-sample indices of the $k$ selected experts}
   210→\STATE $\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\leftarrow \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{g})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Renormalized over selected experts only}
   211→\STATE \textbf{for each}\ $j \in \mathcal{T}$\ \textbf{do}
   212→\STATE \quad $\mathbf{o}_j\leftarrow \text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Only the $k$ selected experts execute}
   213→\STATE \textbf{end for}
   214→\STATE $\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow \sum_{j\in\mathcal{T}}\widetilde{w}_j\cdot \mathbf{o}_j$
   215→\RETURN $\mathbf{Y}$
   216→\end{algorithmic}
   217→\end{algorithm}
   218→
   219→\section{Experiments}
   220→
   221→\subsection{Setup}
   222→
   223→\textbf{Backbone.} MOMENT-small~\citep{goswami2024moment} ($d_\text{model}{=}512$ in the released \texttt{AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small} checkpoint, 8 T5-style encoder blocks). Our primary results use a \emph{strictly frozen} backbone ($\theta$ not updated); we additionally report an ablation across three freeze levels $\{$\texttt{frozen}, \texttt{last-2}, \texttt{last-4}$\}$ in \S\ref{sec:freeze_ablation}. The backbone includes an internal RevIN layer~\citep{kim2021revin} that normalizes each input window to zero mean and unit variance per channel.
   224→
   225→\textbf{MSE scale.} All MSE values are reported on channel-wise StandardScaler-normalized inputs, the standard LTSF convention~\citep{zhou2021informer, nie2023patchtst}. As a calibration anchor, DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} on our pipeline achieves $0.416$ (ETTh1), $0.322$ (ETTm1), $0.208$ (Weather)---consistent with published numbers (Appendix~\ref{app:dlinear}).
   226→
   227→\textbf{Datasets and tasks.} ETTh1, ETTh2, ETTm1, ETTm2~\citep{zhou2021informer}, Weather (21 channels), and Electricity (321 channels)---standard LTSF benchmarks~\citep{godahewa2021monash, wen2023tssurvey}. Chronological train/val/test splits follow the LTSF protocol. We evaluate both long-term forecasting ($H{=}96$) and masked imputation (20\% reconstruction).
   228→
   229→\textbf{Training.} Adam optimizer, lr$=10^{-3}$, MSE loss, batch size 128, 15 epochs. Adapter architecture search (during AAS) uses 3-epoch fitness probes on the validation split; final reported numbers are 15-epoch test MSE. Multi-seed experiments use seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$.
   230→
   231→\textbf{Fixed adapter baselines.} Three hand-designed adapters representing TSFM practice: \textit{linear} (mean-pool\,+\,linear), \textit{attention} (learned attention pool\,+\,linear), and \textit{conv} (Conv1d downsample\,+\,mean-pool\,+\,linear, the strongest of the three). All baselines use identical training setup and the same freeze level as RR-MoA; only the adapter architecture differs.
   232→
   233→\textbf{Reproducibility.} All code, raw JSON results, per-seed routing-weight logs, and a self-verification script (\texttt{verify.py}) that re-derives every numerical claim from raw data are included in the supplementary material and will be open-sourced upon acceptance.
   234→
   235→\subsection{Main Results: AAS and Cross-Backbone}
   236→\label{sec:main_results}
   237→
   238→AAS wins all 20 single-adapter experiments on MOMENT-small across 6 datasets and horizons H=96--720 (average $-9.0\%$ MSE vs.\ best fixed adapter; full grid in Appendix~\ref{app:aas_results}, Table~\ref{tab:main}). AAS also generalizes across backbones: it wins on MOMENT-large ($d_\text{model}{=}1024$, 24 blocks) and Moirai-1.1-R-small, with improvements of $-3.0\%$ to $-18.0\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_results}, Table~\ref{tab:backbone}). AAS is used here primarily to populate the RR-MoA expert pool; the core contribution is the routing framework below.
   239→
   240→\subsection{Search Space Decomposition and Expert Pool}
   241→\label{sec:architectures}
   242→
   243→A factorial decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:decomposition}) confirms that the code space is the dominant factor: LLM-discovered adapters achieve $19.6\%$ lower MSE than template-space adapters ($p{=}0.003$, Cohen's $d{=}0.67$). AAS recovers cross-domain motifs---depthwise convolutions~\citep{howard2017mobilenets}, Conv1d+BatchNorm~\citep{he2016resnet, ioffe2015batchnorm}, feature attention~\citep{hu2018senet}---absent from any published TSFM adapter (Appendix~\ref{app:architectures}). To isolate routing efficacy from expert capacity, we distill the AAS logs into five lightweight archetypes (mean, last-token, max, attention, Conv1d pooling) used as the fixed expert pool in all RR-MoA experiments.
   244→
   245→\subsection{RR-MoA: Routing Collapse and Its Resolution}
   246→\label{sec:freeze_ablation}
   247→
   248→Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} presents the central experimental result of the paper. Using the \emph{same five expert adapter heads} (mean, last-token, max, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool) in all three rows, we compare Top-2 RR-MoA against the single best fixed adapter baseline across a full grid of 3 datasets $\times$ 3 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds. RR-MoA wins \textbf{27 out of 27} configurations with tight standard deviations.

   250→\begin{table}[h]

   252→\caption{\textbf{Multi-seed freeze-level ablation (test MSE, mean\,$\pm$\,std over seeds $\{42,43,44\}$, H=96).} \textsc{RR-MoA} uses Top-2 sparse routing over the same 5 canonical experts; the ``best fixed'' column is the best of $\{$linear, attention, conv$\}$ trained identically. RR-MoA wins \textbf{54/54} configurations across 6 datasets (extended grid: ETTh2, ETTm2, Electricity in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}); the improvement is largest under the strictly frozen backbone (``Frozen Paradox'').}
   253→\label{tab:rrmoa}
   254→\small
   255→\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
   256→\toprule
   257→Dataset & Freeze level & RR-MoA (Top-2) & Best fixed & $\Delta$\% \\
   258→\midrule
   259→\multirow{3}{*}{ETTh1}
   260→ & Frozen (0/8)   & $\mathbf{0.690 \pm 0.021}$ & $1.220 \pm 0.023$ & $\mathbf{-43.5\%}$ \\
   261→ & Last-2 (2/8)   & $\mathbf{0.727 \pm 0.074}$ & $1.030 \pm 0.139$ & $-28.5\%$ \\
   262→ & Last-4 (4/8)   & $\mathbf{0.749 \pm 0.036}$ & $1.101 \pm 0.120$ & $-30.8\%$ \\
   263→\midrule
   264→\multirow{3}{*}{ETTm1}
   265→ & Frozen (0/8)   & $\mathbf{0.572 \pm 0.073}$ & $1.169 \pm 0.006$ & $\mathbf{-51.1\%}$ \\
   266→ & Last-2 (2/8)   & $\mathbf{0.623 \pm 0.032}$ & $0.891 \pm 0.049$ & $-29.8\%$ \\
   267→ & Last-4 (4/8)   & $\mathbf{0.571 \pm 0.034}$ & $0.866 \pm 0.016$ & $-34.1\%$ \\
   268→\midrule
   269→\multirow{3}{*}{Weather}
   270→ & Frozen (0/8)   & $\mathbf{0.289 \pm 0.008}$ & $0.522 \pm 0.003$ & $-44.6\%$ \\
   271→ & Last-2 (2/8)   & $\mathbf{0.251 \pm 0.005}$ & $0.478 \pm 0.025$ & $-47.2\%$ \\
   272→ & Last-4 (4/8)   & $\mathbf{0.256 \pm 0.014}$ & $0.497 \pm 0.033$ & $\mathbf{-48.0\%}$ \\
   273→\bottomrule
   274→\end{tabular}
   275→\end{table}
   276→
   277→\textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} On ETTh1 and ETTm1, the \emph{strictly frozen} backbone yields RR-MoA's best absolute MSE; on Weather, last-2 is marginally better ($-47.2\%$ vs $-44.6\%$). Across all 6 datasets (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}), strictly frozen is the best configuration on 4 and within $13\%$ on the remaining 2, \emph{reversing} the standard NLP PEFT heuristic. The mechanism is Table~\ref{tab:adamix}: unfreezing the backbone is precisely what destroys routing diversity.
   278→
   279→\textbf{Routing vs.\ ensembling.} Replacing the learned router with a fixed uniform mixture ($w_k = 1/K$) degrades performance by $30$--$75\%$ on every dataset (ETTh1: $0.899$ vs $0.690$; ETTm1: $1.002$ vs $0.572$; Weather: $0.494$ vs $0.289$), confirming that the router makes meaningful per-sample decisions.
   280→
   281→\begin{table}[h]
   282→\centering
   283→\caption{\textbf{Routing collapse control: AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} with identical experts.} Routing entropy is the Shannon entropy of the mean routing distribution (max $\log 5 \approx 1.609$). Under unfreezing, entropy drops to $0.000\pm 0.000$---all weight on one expert. Extended grid (ETTh2, ETTm2, Electricity) in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}.}
   284→\label{tab:adamix}
   285→\small
   286→\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
   287→\toprule
   288→Dataset & Freeze level & MSE (mean$\pm$std) & Routing entropy (mean$\pm$std) \\
   289→\midrule
   290→\multirow{3}{*}{ETTh1}
   291→ & Frozen  & $1.105 \pm 0.026$ & $0.629 \pm 0.436$ \\
   292→ & Last-2  & $1.153 \pm 0.000$ & $\mathbf{0.000 \pm 0.000}$ \\
   293→ & Last-4  & $1.154 \pm 0.001$ & $\mathbf{0.000 \pm 0.000}$ \\
   294→\midrule
   295→\multirow{3}{*}{ETTm1}
   296→ & Frozen  & $1.008 \pm 0.012$ & $0.487 \pm 0.371$ \\
   297→ & Last-2  & $1.061 \pm 0.088$ & $0.218 \pm 0.309$ \\
   298→ & Last-4  & $1.123 \pm 0.000$ & $\mathbf{0.000 \pm 0.000}$ \\
   299→\midrule
   300→\multirow{3}{*}{Weather}
   301→ & Frozen  & $0.459 \pm 0.017$ & $0.509 \pm 0.307$ \\
   302→ & Last-2  & $0.607 \pm 0.002$ & $\mathbf{0.000 \pm 0.000}$ \\
   303→ & Last-4  & $0.607 \pm 0.002$ & $\mathbf{0.000 \pm 0.000}$ \\
   304→\bottomrule
   305→\end{tabular}
   306→\end{table}
   307→
   308→\textbf{AdaMix} collapses to a single expert whenever backbone layers are unfrozen: routing entropy drops to exactly $0.000\pm0.000$ in 5 of 12 unfrozen configurations, and remains below $0.55$ in most others. Under the strictly frozen setting entropy recovers ($0.3$--$1.0$), but MSE is still dominated by RR-MoA. Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory} provides per-step mechanistic evidence: under unfreezing (red), entropy collapses within ${\sim}50$ gradient steps as the dominant expert's gradient norm grows and others starve; under freezing (blue), entropy remains stable near $1.5$.
   309→
   310→\begin{figure}[h]
   311→\centering
   312→\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/adamix_trajectory.pdf}
   313→\caption{\textbf{Per-step routing / gradient trajectory for AdaMix on ETTh1.} (a)~Router entropy over optimizer steps: unfrozen backbone (red) collapses from near-uniform (${\approx}\log 5$) to ${\approx}0$ within ${\sim}50$ steps; frozen backbone (blue) remains stable near $1.5$. (b)~Per-expert gradient L2 norm (unfrozen only): the dominant expert's gradient grows while the others starve, confirming the gradient co-adaptation feedback loop of \S\ref{sec:rrmoa}. This trajectory directly visualizes the mechanism behind Table~\ref{tab:adamix}'s endpoint entropies.}
   314→\label{fig:trajectory}
   315→\end{figure}
   316→
   317→\textbf{RR-MoA} maintains routing entropy $1.0$--$1.57$ (max $1.609$) across all 54 configurations and wins \textbf{54/54} against the best fixed adapter, with improvements from $-18\%$ to $-77\%$. The contrast with AdaMix is stark: raw-signal routing is immune to gradient co-adaptation.
   318→
   319→\textbf{Comparison to LoRA.} We add LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} under the identical strictly frozen backbone protocol, sweeping $r{\in}\{8,16,32\}$, targets ${\in}\{q{+}v, qkvo\}$, and heads ${\in}\{$linear, MLP$\}$ (108 runs per dataset; Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}).
   320→
   321→\begin{table}[h]
   322→\centering
   323→\caption{\textbf{Modern PEFT baseline comparison (strictly frozen backbone, 3 seeds, test MSE mean$\pm$std).} ``Best LoRA'' is the strongest configuration from a sweep over $r{\in}\{8,16,32\}$, targets ${\in}\{q{+}v,\; q{+}k{+}v{+}o\}$, and heads ${\in}\{$linear, MLP$\}$ (12 configs $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ 108 runs per dataset; full grid in Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}). Even the best LoRA variant fails to close the gap to RR-MoA, confirming that low-rank backbone perturbations alone cannot unlock more downstream signal from a frozen TSFM. RR-MoA (Top-2 sparse) wins all head-to-head configurations, with $40$--$52\%$ MSE reductions.}
   324→\label{tab:lora}
   325→\small
   326→\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
   327→\toprule
   328→Dataset & Best fixed & Best LoRA (config) & RR-MoA (Top-2) & $\Delta$ (RR-MoA vs LoRA) \\
   329→\midrule
   330→ETTh1   & $1.220 \pm 0.023$ & $1.154 \pm 0.000$ {\scriptsize($r{=}32$, qkvo, mlp)} & $\mathbf{0.690 \pm 0.021}$ & $\mathbf{-40.2\%}$ \\
   331→ETTm1   & $1.169 \pm 0.006$ & $0.956 \pm 0.072$ {\scriptsize($r{=}16$, qkvo, lin)} & $\mathbf{0.572 \pm 0.073}$ & $-40.2\%$ \\
   332→Weather & $0.522 \pm 0.003$ & $0.600 \pm 0.052$ {\scriptsize($r{=}32$, qv, lin)} & $\mathbf{0.289 \pm 0.008}$ & $-51.8\%$ \\
   333→\bottomrule
   334→\end{tabular}
   335→\end{table}
   336→
   337→Even the strongest LoRA configuration from a 108-run sweep (Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}) fails to close the gap: low-rank perturbations do not meaningfully reshape the frozen backbone's feature geometry. RR-MoA delivers $40$--$52\%$ MSE reductions over the best LoRA by investing capacity in routing over topologically distinct experts based on the raw pre-normalization signal.
   338→
   339→\textbf{Supervised calibration anchor.} DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} trained from scratch on our exact pipeline achieves $0.416{\pm}0.002$ (ETTh1), $0.322{\pm}0.004$ (ETTm1), $0.208{\pm}0.003$ (Weather)---roughly $40$--$60\%$ below our best frozen-backbone RR-MoA. This gap is expected; our contribution is the best routing strategy \emph{within} the frozen-backbone paradigm, not absolute SOTA.
   340→
   341→\textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass.} To isolate whether RR-MoA's advantage comes from bypassing the backbone or specifically from reading the raw pre-normalization signal, we replace the router's input with the same signal after per-window RevIN (zero mean, unit variance). Everything else is identical.
   342→
   343→\begin{table}[h]
   344→\centering
   345→\caption{\textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass ablation} (strictly frozen backbone, Top-2 sparse routing, 3 seeds, test MSE mean$\pm$std). The raw router reads the channel-standardized input signal; the RevIN router reads the same signal after an additional per-window zero-mean unit-variance transform that strips temporal statistics. The final column reports relative MSE \emph{degradation} of the RevIN router against the raw router --- \textbf{positive values mean the raw router is better}. Stripping the rawness degrades MSE by 60--88\% across all three datasets, confirming that routing on the backbone's post-normalization representation is insufficient: the per-window trend/amplitude/volatility that the normalization cascade removes are precisely the statistics the router relies on.}
   346→\label{tab:router_input}
   347→\small
   348→\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
   349→\toprule
   350→Dataset & Raw router (main) & RevIN router & Degradation vs Raw ($\Delta\%$, $\uparrow$ worse) \\
   351→\midrule
   352→ETTh1   & $\mathbf{0.690 \pm 0.021}$ & $1.101 \pm 0.008$ & $\mathbf{+59.6\%}$ \\
   353→ETTm1   & $\mathbf{0.572 \pm 0.073}$ & $1.077 \pm 0.020$ & $\mathbf{+88.3\%}$ \\
   354→Weather & $\mathbf{0.289 \pm 0.008}$ & $0.542 \pm 0.026$ & $\mathbf{+87.5\%}$ \\
   355→\bottomrule
   356→\end{tabular}
   357→\end{table}
   358→
   359→The RevIN router suffers $60$--$88\%$ MSE degradation on every dataset: bypass alone is insufficient; the \emph{content} of the raw signal matters. Routing entropy remains high ($1.50$--$1.57$), but the gate can no longer make discriminative decisions without per-window temporal statistics.
   360→
   361→\subsection{Top-\texorpdfstring{$k$}{k} Sparse Routing and Task Generalization}
   362→\label{sec:topk}
   363→
   364→Top-2 sparse routing retains the vast majority of the dense mixture's gain while activating only $2$ of $5$ experts per sample, bounding active expert FLOPs to $40\%$ of the dense setting (full ablation in Appendix~\ref{app:topk}). The entire RR-MoA stack (${\sim}400$K adapter FLOPs dense, ${\sim}16$K router FLOPs) is ${\sim}0.003\%$ of a single MOMENT-small forward pass, so inference overhead is negligible. RR-MoA also generalizes to imputation (20\% masked reconstruction), achieving $-43\%$ to $-69\%$ MSE improvement over the best single head on 3/3 datasets (Appendix~\ref{app:imputation}).
   365→
   366→
   367→\section{Conclusion}
   368→
   369→We identified a gradient co-adaptation failure mode in MoE adaptation of pretrained TSFMs: whenever backbone layers are unfrozen, the router and backbone jointly collapse onto a single expert (routing entropy $0.000\pm0.000$). Our solution, \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}, breaks this loop by (i)~keeping the backbone strictly frozen and (ii)~routing on the raw pre-normalization input signal. A controlled ablation confirms that the \emph{rawness}---the per-window trend, amplitude, and volatility---is essential: stripping it via RevIN degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}). RR-MoA wins \textbf{54/54} configurations across six datasets, three freeze levels, and three seeds. The \emph{Frozen Paradox}---that the strictly frozen backbone is the best or near-best strategy for routing-based adaptation---reverses the standard NLP PEFT heuristic of unfreezing the top encoder blocks. Top-$k$ sparse routing bounds active expert FLOPs to $40\%$ of a dense mixture while retaining the gains (\S\ref{sec:topk}). To populate the expert pool we use \textbf{AAS}, an LLM-guided code-level search that imports cross-domain motifs into the TSFM adapter vocabulary (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_results}).
   370→
   371→\textbf{Limitations.} (1)~Absolute MSE is materially higher than supervised baselines (DLinear achieves $0.416$ on ETTh1 vs.\ our $0.690$); our contribution is the best routing strategy within the frozen-backbone paradigm, not absolute SOTA. (2)~Discrete-tokenized backbones (Chronos) and native MoE TSFMs (Moirai-MoE, Time-MoE) are not addressed; we leave these to future work.

A parameter-normalized variant (e.g.\ $\|\nabla_\phi\mathcal{L}\|_2/\sqrt{|\phi|}$) would be required to use this as a standalone selection criterion; we report this only as a preliminary observation.}

   910→\section{Proposition Proofs}
   911→\label{app:proofs}
   912→
   913→\textbf{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:hierarchy}} (Strict Head-Level Hierarchy: $\mathcal{C}_\text{head} \subset \mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl} \subset \mathcal{G}_\text{code}$).
   914→
   915→\begin{proof}
   916→\emph{$\mathcal{C}_\text{head} \subseteq \mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$}: Each $c \in \mathcal{C}_\text{head}$ is, by Definition~\ref{def:discrete_head}, a pooling strategy followed by an MLP head. Both components are expressible as template derivations of the form $S \to \text{Pool}\cdot\text{MLP}\cdot\text{Output}$ (Appendix~\ref{app:grammar}), so $c$ lies in $\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$. \emph{Strict}: $\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$ additionally admits Conv1d pooling and SiLU activations absent from $\mathcal{C}_\text{head}$.
   917→
   918→\emph{$\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl} \subseteq \mathcal{G}_\text{code}$}: Every template derivation produces a valid Python program satisfying Definition~\ref{def:code}. \emph{Strict}: $\mathcal{G}_\text{code}$ contains depthwise convolutions, batch normalization, residual connections, and learned positional weights---none derivable from the template grammar (Table~\ref{tab:expressiveness}).
   919→
   920→\emph{Orthogonality of LoRA and bottleneck configurations.} Configurations in $\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{C}_\text{head}$ modify the backbone's attention projections (LoRA) or inject residual modules between encoder blocks (bottleneck). They are not head-level adapters and are therefore not members of $\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$ nor of $\mathcal{G}_\text{code}$ as defined (the code space is restricted to adapter classes operating on extracted hidden states, per Definition~\ref{def:code}). We compare against the LoRA variant separately under the strictly frozen backbone protocol in Table~\ref{tab:lora}.
   921→\end{proof}
   922→
   923→\textbf{Budget Sufficiency (Empirical).} Figure~\ref{fig:budget} shows that all 14 tested search budgets $B\in[20,480]$ beat the conv baseline on both ETTh1 and ETTm1 and that the MSE as a function of $B$ saturates around $B\!\approx\!60$. Evolutionary selection did not outperform random sampling in the template space $\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$, indicating that the returns to additional samples past the saturation point are negligible for this space. We report this purely as an empirical observation from the curve; no theoretical sample-complexity bound is claimed.
   924→
   925→\section{Template Grammar Production Rules}
   926→\label{app:grammar}
   927→
   928→\begin{align*}
   929→S &\rightarrow \nonterminal{Pool} \;\cdot\; \nonterminal{MLP} \;\cdot\; \nonterminal{Output} \\
   930→\nonterminal{Pool} &\rightarrow \text{MeanPool} \mid \text{MaxPool} \mid \text{LastToken} \mid \text{AttnPool} \mid \text{Conv1dPool} \\
   931→\nonterminal{MLP} &\rightarrow \nonterminal{Layer} \mid \nonterminal{Layer} \;\cdot\; \nonterminal{MLP} \quad (\text{depth} \leq 3) \\
   932→\nonterminal{Layer} &\rightarrow \text{Linear}(d_\text{in}, d_\text{out}) \;\cdot\; \nonterminal{Act} \;\cdot\; \text{Dropout}(p) \\
   933→\nonterminal{Act} &\rightarrow \text{GELU} \mid \text{ReLU} \mid \text{SiLU} \mid \text{Tanh} \\
   934→\nonterminal{Output} &\rightarrow \text{Linear}(d_\text{last}, H)
   935→\end{align*}
   936→with $d_\text{out} \in \{64, 128, 192, 256, 384\}$ and $p \in \{0.0, 0.1, 0.2, 0.3\}$, yielding $|\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}| \approx 1{,}200$.
   937→

   999→\section{Formal Definitions}
  1000→\label{app:formal_defs}
  1001→
  1002→\begin{definition}[Adapter]
  1003→Given a pretrained TSFM backbone $f_\theta: \mathbb{R}^{B \times L} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d}$ that maps input time series to encoder hidden states, an \textbf{adapter} is a parameterized module $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$ that maps hidden states to forecasts, where $\phi \in \Phi_g \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{n_g}$ are trainable weights with $n_g \leq N_\text{max}$ (we set $N_\text{max} = 500{,}000$).
  1004→\end{definition}
  1005→
  1006→\begin{definition}[Architecture Space]
  1007→An \textbf{architecture space} $\mathcal{G}$ is a set of adapter specifications, where each $g \in \mathcal{G}$ determines: (i) a parameter space $\Phi_g$ and its dimensionality $n_g$, (ii) a computational graph defining the forward function $g_\phi(\cdot)$, and (iii) an initialization procedure for $\phi$.
  1008→\end{definition}
  1009→
  1010→\begin{definition}[Discrete Configuration Space $\mathcal{C}$]
  1011→The \textbf{discrete space} $\mathcal{C}$ is the Cartesian product of standard adapter hyperparameters:
  1012→\begin{equation}
  1013→\mathcal{C} = \underbrace{\{\text{lin, lora, bn}\}}_{\text{type}} \times \underbrace{\{8,16,32,64\}}_{\text{rank}} \times \underbrace{\{\text{mean, last, max, cls}\}}_{\text{pool}} \times \underbrace{\{\text{lin, mlp1, mlp2}\}}_{\text{head}} \times \cdots
  1014→\end{equation}
  1015→The full Cartesian product yields ${\sim}2{,}160$ configurations, of which ${\sim}144$ define distinct adapter \textit{computational graphs} when restricted to the head-only subspace.
  1016→\end{definition}
  1017→
  1018→\begin{definition}[Head-Only Subspace $\mathcal{C}_\text{head}$]
  1019→\label{def:discrete_head}
  1020→The \textbf{head-only subspace} $\mathcal{C}_\text{head} \subset \mathcal{C}$ is the restriction of $\mathcal{C}$ to linear-probe configurations (pooling $\to$ MLP head), with LoRA rank $=0$ and no bottleneck.
  1021→\end{definition}
  1022→
  1023→\begin{definition}[Code-Level Space $\mathcal{G}_\text{code}$]
  1024→\label{def:code}
  1025→The \textbf{code space} $\mathcal{G}_\text{code}$ is the set of all Python programs $p$ such that: (i)~$p$ defines a class \texttt{Adapter(nn.Module)} with \texttt{\_\_init\_\_(self, d\_model, output\_dim)} and \texttt{forward(self, h)} mapping $\mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$; (ii)~$n_p \leq N_\text{max}$; (iii)~$p$ uses only \texttt{\{torch, nn, F, math\}}.
  1026→\end{definition}
  1027→
  1028→\begin{definition}[Template Space $\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$]
  1029→\label{def:template}
  1030→The \textbf{template space} $\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl} \subset \mathcal{G}_\text{code}$ is generated by a context-free grammar over 5 pooling types, 4 activations, variable MLP depths and widths, yielding $|\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}| \approx 1{,}200$ architectures (Appendix~\ref{app:grammar}).
  1031→\end{definition}
  1032→
  1033→\begin{proposition}[Strict Head-Level Hierarchy]
  1034→\label{prop:hierarchy}
  1035→$\mathcal{C}_\text{head} \subset \mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl} \subset \mathcal{G}_\text{code}$, and both inclusions are strict (proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}).
  1036→\end{proposition}
  1037→
  1038→\section{Search Space Expressiveness}
  1039→\label{app:expressiveness}
  1040→
  1041→\begin{table}[h]
  1042→\centering
  1043→\caption{Search space expressiveness hierarchy. Winning AAS architectures lie in $\mathcal{G}_\text{code} \setminus \mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$.}
  1044→\label{tab:expressiveness}
  1045→\small
  1046→\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
  1047→\toprule
  1048→Architecture & Discrete $\mathcal{C}$ & Template $\mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl}$ & Code $\mathcal{G}_\text{code}$ & Source \\
  1049→\midrule
  1050→Linear probe (mean pool + linear) & \cmark & \cmark & \cmark & Standard \\
  1051→MLP head (1--2 layer, GELU) & \cmark & \cmark & \cmark & Standard \\
  1052→Conv1d pool + MLP & \xmark & \cmark & \cmark & Template \\
  1053→\midrule
  1054→\multicolumn{5}{l}{\textit{Head-level hierarchy: }$\mathcal{C}_\text{head} \subset \mathcal{G}_\text{tmpl} \subset \mathcal{G}_\text{code}$} \\
  1055→LoRA ($r$, $\alpha$, targets) on backbone & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\emph{orthogonal axis; see Table~\ref{tab:lora}}} & Backbone PEFT \\
  1056→Bottleneck adapter between blocks & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\emph{orthogonal axis}} & Backbone PEFT \\
  1057→\midrule
  1058→Conv1d + BatchNorm + residual & \xmark & \xmark & \cmark & \textbf{Discovered} \\
  1059→Depthwise conv + positional weights & \xmark & \xmark & \cmark & \textbf{Discovered} \\
  1060→Softmax feature attention + gating & \xmark & \xmark & \cmark & \textbf{Discovered} \\
  1061→Multi-scale conv + residual & \xmark & \xmark & \cmark & \textbf{Discovered} \\
  1062→\bottomrule
  1063→\end{tabular}
  1064→\end{table}

  1066→\section{AAS Main Results}
  1067→\label{app:aas_results}
  1068→
  1069→\begin{table}[h]
  1070→\centering
  1071→\caption{Standard LTSF benchmark on MOMENT-small (test MSE, 3 seeds unless noted). AAS wins all 20 experiments. Best fixed adapter is \textit{conv}. AAS is used primarily to populate the RR-MoA expert pool (\S\ref{sec:architectures}).}
  1072→\label{tab:main}
  1073→\small
  1074→\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
  1075→\toprule
  1076→Dataset & Setting & AAS MSE & Best Fixed & $\Delta$ \\
  1077→\midrule

  1218→
  1219→\section{Factorial Decomposition}
  1220→\label{app:decomposition}
  1221→
  1222→We decompose the contributions of three factors across 6 evaluation datasets: (1) search space representation (code vs.\ discrete), (2) evolutionary selection pressure, and (3) LLM guidance.
  1223→
  1224→\textbf{Code space $\gg$ discrete space.} All code-space methods outperform discrete evolutionary search (Evo-3epoch, 144 distinct adapter graphs) on 6/6 datasets (Wilcoxon signed-rank $p{=}0.031$, Cohen's $d{=}0.82$). The search space representation is the dominant factor.
  1225→
  1226→\textbf{LLM patterns are individually superior.} Within AAS runs, adapters containing LLM-discovered patterns achieve 19.6\% lower MSE than basic architectures ($p{=}0.003$, Mann-Whitney $U$, $n{=}90$, Cohen's $d{=}0.67$). LLM adapters also use 38\% fewer parameters (mean 110K vs.\ 177K).
  1227→
  1228→\textbf{Validity determines aggregate winner.} The LLM wastes 25\% of evaluations on invalid code; templates achieve 100\% validity.
  1229→
  1230→\textbf{Search budget sufficiency.} We tested 14 budget configurations (20--480 evaluations) across ETTh1 and ETTm1; all beat the conv baseline, even at $B{=}20$ (Appendix~\ref{app:budget}). The curve plateaus around $B\!\approx\!60$.
  1231→

  1291→
  1292→\section{Deployment-Regime Motivation}
  1293→\label{app:deployment}
  1294→
  1295→The frozen-backbone paradigm is motivated by three deployment regimes where full fine-tuning is infeasible:
  1296→
  1297→\textbf{(i) Multi-tenant cloud serving.} A single TSFM sits in GPU VRAM and serves thousands of tenants via lightweight, hot-swappable per-tenant adapters in host RAM. Retraining a separate DLinear per tenant does not amortize the backbone cost and forfeits zero-shot transfer.
  1298→
  1299→\textbf{(ii) On-device and edge deployment.} One backbone is shipped with the device image; per-site adapters are updated over low-bandwidth links. Collecting each site's training data centrally is often impossible for privacy or latency reasons.
  1300→
  1301→\textbf{(iii) Cross-task reuse.} The same frozen backbone drives forecasting, imputation, and classification via different expert pools. We demonstrate the first two tasks explicitly in the main text.
  1302→

# NeurIPS 2026 Review: "Raw-Routed Adapters and Architecture Search for Time Series Foundation Models"

## Summary

This paper identifies a gradient co-adaptation failure mode in MoE-style adapter routing for time series foundation models (TSFMs): when backbone layers are unfrozen, routing entropy collapses to 0.000, concentrating all weight on a single expert. The proposed fix, **RR-MoA**, routes on the raw pre-normalization input signal with a strictly frozen backbone, achieving 54/54 wins against fixed single-adapter baselines across 6 datasets, 3 freeze levels, and 3 seeds. An **Adapter Architecture Search (AAS)** component uses LLM-guided code evolution to populate the expert pool. Top-2 sparse routing bounds active FLOPs to 40% of dense.

---

## Scores

| Criterion | Score (1-10) |
|---|---|
| **Soundness** | 5 |
| **Novelty** | 6 |
| **Clarity** | 7 |
| **Significance** | 4 |
| **Reproducibility** | 7 |
| **Overall** | 5 |

**Confidence:** 4/5

**Recommendation:** Borderline Reject (5/10)

---

## Strengths

**S1. Clean problem identification with strong empirical evidence.** The gradient co-adaptation diagnosis is compelling. The routing entropy = 0.000+-0.000 across all unfrozen configurations is a striking, reproducible finding. Figure 2 showing per-step entropy collapse within ~50 gradient steps provides direct mechanistic evidence. This is the strongest part of the paper.

**S2. Well-designed ablations.** The rawness-vs-bypass ablation (Table 5) is excellent experimental design — it cleanly isolates that the *content* of the raw signal (trend, amplitude, volatility) matters, not merely bypassing the backbone. The routing-vs-ensembling comparison (uniform mixture degrades 30-75%) confirms meaningful per-sample decisions. The full LoRA sweep (108 runs/dataset) is thorough.

**S3. Honest limitations section.** The paper explicitly acknowledges the DLinear gap (40-78% worse than supervised) and scopes the contribution to the frozen-backbone paradigm. This intellectual honesty is appreciated.

**S4. Consistent multi-seed evaluation.** 3 seeds throughout with reported standard deviations. The 54/54 win rate against fixed baselines is impressively consistent.

**S5. Good paper structure and clarity.** The narrative flows logically: problem (collapse) → diagnosis (co-adaptation) → fix (RR-MoA) → expert pool (AAS). The architecture diagram (Figure 1) is informative.

---

## Weaknesses

**W1. Extremely weak baselines inflate the claims (Major).** The "best fixed adapter" baselines are three hand-designed heads (mean-pool+linear, attention+linear, conv+linear). These achieve MSE 1.220 on ETTh1 — roughly **3x worse than DLinear (0.416)** trained from scratch. The 54/54 wins and -43% to -77% reductions are against these weak baselines, not against the state of the art in TSFM adaptation. Where are comparisons against:
- MSFT (NeurIPS 2025 — the closest published work on TSFM adaptation)
- TRACE (2025 — importance-based LoRA with reconstructed heads)
- AdaPTS (2025 — input representation adaptation)
- Simple independent adapter ensembles (train K adapters separately, average predictions at test time — the obvious non-routing alternative)

The paper cites all of these in Related Work but benchmarks against none. The LoRA comparison is welcome but insufficient — LoRA is not designed for this frozen-backbone regime.

**W2. Absolute MSE values raise fundamental questions about the paradigm (Major).** Frozen MOMENT-small's best single adapter achieves 1.220 MSE on ETTh1 H=96. DLinear achieves 0.416. Standard LTSF methods (PatchTST, iTransformer) achieve 0.37-0.40. Even RR-MoA's 0.690 is **66% worse than DLinear**. This raises a core question the paper doesn't adequately address: *if the frozen TSFM representations are this poor, what is the practical value of routing over them?* The deployment motivation (multi-tenant, edge, cross-task) is argued theoretically in Appendix K but demonstrated nowhere. Without at least one real deployment scenario, the entire paradigm being optimized for remains hypothetical.

**W3. The "Frozen Paradox" may be an artifact of low-capacity adapters (Major).** The paper claims that frozen > unfrozen for routing is a "paradox" that "reverses the standard NLP PEFT heuristic." But the adapters are simple (mean-pool+linear, etc.). When you unfreeze backbone layers with a low-capacity adapter, the backbone representations can shift into a subspace the adapter exploits, which is exactly gradient co-adaptation. With higher-capacity adapters (e.g., the AAS-discovered ones with depthwise convolutions, 228K params), would unfreezing still collapse? The paper doesn't test this. The "paradox" may simply be: *weak adapters + unfreezing = overfitting*, which is well-known, not paradoxical.

**W4. Disconnection between AAS and RR-MoA (Moderate).** The paper presents AAS as a core contribution, but the actual RR-MoA experiments use 5 hand-curated archetypes "distilled" from AAS logs. This raises several questions:
- Why not use the actual AAS-discovered adapters directly?
- The "distilled" pool uses mean, last-token, max, attention, Conv1d — three of these (mean, last-token, max) are trivial pooling operations expressible in the discrete space. Only attention and Conv1d are non-trivial.
- AAS "recovers" known motifs (MobileNet depthwise conv, ResNet BatchNorm, SE-Net attention). The novelty is applying code search to adapters, but the discovered architectures are well-known building blocks.

**W5. Single backbone for the main contribution (Moderate).** RR-MoA is evaluated exclusively on MOMENT-small. Cross-backbone results in Table 7 are AAS-only (single adapters), not RR-MoA. The routing collapse phenomenon and the frozen paradox may be MOMENT-specific (e.g., due to MOMENT's specific RevIN placement). Without testing on at least one additional backbone (TimesFM, Moirai, Timer), the generalizability claim is unsupported.

**W6. Top-k analysis is misleading (Minor).** Table 8 shows dense (k=5) achieves 0.550 vs Top-2's 0.727 — a **32% MSE increase**. The paper claims Top-2 "retains >94% of the MSE gain." This is computed relative to the baseline gap: (1.030-0.727)/(1.030-0.550) = 63%, not 94%. The 94% figure likely comes from a different baseline or metric. This claim needs correction or clarification.

**W7. Some standard deviations are concerning (Minor).** ETTm1 frozen RR-MoA: 0.572+-0.073 (CV=12.8%). ETTh1 last-2: 0.727+-0.074 (CV=10.2%). With only 3 seeds, these confidence intervals are wide. No formal statistical tests (paired t-tests, Wilcoxon) are reported for the main RR-MoA vs fixed comparisons — only for the factorial decomposition.

**W8. T-ZCP and budget analysis feel like padding (Minor).** Appendix A (T-ZCP) is acknowledged to be confounded by parameter count and is "preliminary." Appendix B (budget scaling) shows random sampling matches evolutionary search. Both detract from rather than support the main narrative.

---

## Questions for Authors

1. Can you provide a comparison against at least MSFT and an independent ensemble baseline (train 5 adapters independently, average predictions)?
2. What happens to routing collapse when the adapter pool contains higher-capacity adapters (e.g., the actual AAS winners with 200K+ params)?
3. Can you replicate the routing collapse / frozen paradox on any backbone other than MOMENT-small?
4. The Top-2 "retains >94% of MSE gain" claim — can you show the exact calculation?
5. Why do frozen MOMENT representations perform so poorly (1.220 MSE with best adapter on ETTh1) compared to published MOMENT results?

---

## Minor Issues

- Line 52: "27/27 wins" in the abstract, but the paper later claims 54/54 (6 datasets). The abstract only counts 3 datasets × 3 freeze × 3 seeds = 27. This is confusing — clarify early.
- Table 6 (AAS main results): ETTh2/ETTm2 MSE values (2.47-3.13) are an order of magnitude higher than ETTh1/ETTm1. Is this a different normalization? Should note this.
- The imputation results (Appendix J) are single-seed only. Given the high variance in forecasting results, this is insufficient.
- Several bibliography entries use "et al." in the author field (e.g., \bibitem[Li et~al.(2022)]{li2022onenas} — "Yonggang Li et al.") which is non-standard for NeurIPS.

---

## Verdict

The paper identifies a genuine and interesting phenomenon (MoE routing collapse on TSFMs) and proposes a clean fix (raw-signal routing + frozen backbone). The ablations isolating rawness vs bypass are well-designed. However, the evaluation is seriously weakened by (1) extremely weak baselines that inflate the improvement numbers, (2) no comparison against any published TSFM adaptation method, (3) evaluation on a single backbone, and (4) absolute MSE values so poor that the practical value of the frozen-backbone paradigm remains undemonstrated. The AAS component, while technically sound, is disconnected from the main contribution and recovers known architectural motifs. In its current form, the paper demonstrates a routing trick that works well against toy baselines on one backbone but does not convincingly advance the state of TSFM adaptation.

     7→**Rule:** When making claims about RR-MoA superiority, ensure baselines include at least one published TSFM adaptation method (MSFT, TRACE, AdaPTS) — not just hand-designed heads and LoRA.
     8→
     9→**Why:** Reviewer W1 notes that 54/54 wins against 3 trivial baselines (mean-pool+linear, attention+linear, conv+linear) inflates claims. No comparison against any method from Related Work.
    10→
    11→**How to apply:**
    12→- Before finalizing paper: add MSFT and/or TRACE comparison, or at minimum an independent ensemble baseline
    13→- Frame frozen-backbone as a specific deployment regime, not the general case
    14→- Verify Top-k retention claims carefully (dense=0.550 vs top-2=0.727 math needs checking)
    15→- Run RR-MoA on at least one non-MOMENT backbone to validate generalization
    16→- Add formal statistical tests (paired t-test or Wilcoxon) for main comparisons
    17→
```

Perfect! Now I have comprehensive coverage of the codebase. Let me create a detailed summary document.

## Summary: RR-MoA Experiment Infrastructure

Based on my thorough exploration of the codebase, here's what the current infrastructure supports:

### 1. **`scripts/run_rr_moa.py` - CLI Arguments & Configuration**

**Key CLI arguments:**
- `--dataset` (default: "ETTh1") — dataset name
- `--horizon` (default: 96) — forecast horizon
- `--K` (default: 5) — number of experts in the mixture
- `--top-k` (default: None = dense) — sparse routing; supports [1, 2, 3, None]
- `--router-input-mode` — signal the router reads:
  - `"raw"` (default) — raw standardized input (core innovation)
  - `"revin"` — RevIN-normalized input (ablation, tests rawness)
  - `"uniform"` — fixed 1/K weights, no routing (ensemble-vs-specialization control, T1.B)
- `--unfreeze` — backbone freeze strategy: `"frozen"` (default), `"last2"`, `"last4"`, `"all"`
- `--expert-pool` — expert pool selection:
  - `"canonical"` (default) — 5 simple pooling heads: mean, last, max, attention, conv1d
  - `"macro"` — 5 AAS-discovered cross-domain architectures (T3.A integration)
- `--seed` (default: 42), `--epochs` (default: 15), `--device` (default: "cuda")
- `--no-baselines` — skip baseline evaluation (faster ablations)
- `--backbone` (default: "AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small") — backbone model name

**Supports baseline adapters via `SEED_ADAPTERS`:**
- "linear", "attention", "conv" (3 hand-designed adapters from code evolution)

### 2. **`feasibility/rrmoa_macro_experts.py` - The 5 Expert Classes**

The macro expert pool contains 5 AAS-discovered architectures, all following the contract `__init__(d_model, output_dim, hidden=None) -> forward(hidden_states)`:

1. **BNMeanLinearExpert** (rank 0, params=50272)
   - BatchNorm1d over d_model → mean pool → linear
   - Inspired by BN's stabilization effect

2. **MultiScaleConvResidualExpert** (rank 1, params=104992)
   - Multi-scale convolutions (k=3,5,7) averaged with BN residual
   - Captures features at different frequency scales

3. **Conv1dBNResidualExpert** (rank 2, params=38688) ← best validation MSE=0.5640
   - Conv1d + BatchNorm + 1×1 residual (ResNet-style)
   - Strong gradient flow via residual connections

4. **DepthwiseSeparableExpert** (rank 3, params=20768) ← smallest
   - Depthwise separable convolutions (MobileNet motif)
   - Parameter-efficient local pattern capture

5. **GatedConvResidualExpert** (rank 4, params=55584)
   - Sigmoid-gated convolution + residual (highway/GLU motif)
   - Adaptive gating between skip and transformation

All validated on ETTh1 seed 42 via `results/code_evolution/validated_ETTh1_42_gpt-4o.json`.

### 3. **`feasibility/model.py` - Backbone Support**

**Supported backbones via `load_backbone(backbone_name, device)`:**

1. **MOMENT** (default)
   - Naming pattern: `"*MOMENT*"` or `"*moment*"` (case-insensitive)
   - Variants supported: small, base, large (via `MOMENTPipeline.from_pretrained`)
   - Default: `"AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small"`
   - Hidden dim extraction: checks `config.d_model`, fallback to inspection

2. **Chronos (T5-based)**
   - Naming pattern: `"*chronos*"`
   - Supports T5 encoder access via `pipe.model.model`
   - Loads via `ChronosPipeline.from_pretrained`

3. **Moirai**
   - Naming pattern: `"*moirai*"`
   - Loads via `MoiraiModule.from_pretrained`
   - Has `.encoder` attribute with `.layers`

**NO explicit support for TimesFM** — would need to be added via new case in `load_backbone()`.

**Encoder block discovery:** `_get_encoder_blocks(model)` tries:
- `m.encoder.block` (MOMENT primary)
- `m.model.encoder.block` (MOMENT variant)
- `m.backbone.encoder.block` (MOMENT variant)
- `m.encoder.layers` (Moirai TransformerEncoder)
- `m.encoder.layer` (generic transformer)
- Fallback: search named_modules for 'block' or 'layers'

**Hidden dimension discovery:** `_get_hidden_dim(model)` checks:
- `config.d_model` or `config.hidden_size`
- Direct model attributes
- Fallback: inspect first Linear layer's input features

### 4. **`feasibility/finetune.py` - Training Loop & Configuration**

**Key training functions:**

- `finetune_forecasting(adapter_cfg, samples, device, n_epochs=10, lr=1e-3, forecast_horizon=96, batch_size=64)` → `Dict[str, float]` with "mse"

**Configurable via `AdapterConfig`:**
- `adapter_type` — "linear_probe", "lora", "bottleneck"
- `lora_rank`, `lora_alpha` — LoRA tuning parameters
- `target_modules` — attention projection layers ("q", "k", "v", "o")
- `layer_placement` — "all", "first_half", "last_half"
- `unfreeze` — backbone freeze strategy
- `head_type` — "linear", "mlp1", "mlp2"
- `pooling` — "mean", "max", "last", "cls_mean_max"

**Feature extraction:** `_extract_features_batch(model, encoder_blocks, batch_x, input_mask, backbone_type)` supports:
- **"moment"**: calls `model(x_enc=batch_x, input_mask=input_mask)`
- **"chronos"**: T5 encoder with simple linear projection hack
- **"moirai"**: patches + projection into model.encoder

### 5. **`scripts/run_freeze_ablation.py` - Orchestration**

This is the main ablation runner with selective experiment modes:

**Supported experiments:**
- `--experiment freeze` (default) — freeze-level x dataset grid: 3 datasets × 3 freeze levels
- `--experiment topk` — sparsity ablation: [top1, top2, top3, dense] on ETTh1
- `--experiment adamix` — routing collapse control (hidden-state routing baseline)
- `--experiment uniform` — T1.B ensemble-vs-specialization control (uniform router)
- `--experiment dlinear` — T1.A DLinear reference (supervised baseline)
- `--experiment macro` — T3.A macro expert pool comparison
- `--experiment all` — run everything in sequence

**Customizable via CLI:**
- `--datasets` — comma-separated (default: ETTh1,ETTm1,Weather)
- `--freeze-levels` — comma-separated (default: frozen,last2,last4)
- `--seed`, `--epochs`, `--device`

**Also launches (via subprocess):**
- `run_adamix.py` — AdaMix routing (hidden-state routing)
- `run_dlinear_baseline.py` — DLinear supervised baseline
- `run_lora_sweep.py` — T3.B LoRA sweep

### 6. **Baseline Infrastructure**

**Existing baselines (NOT MSFT or TRACE in official implementation):**

1. **DLinear** (`scripts/run_dlinear_baseline.py`)
   - Minimal supervised baseline: single Linear(input_len → horizon)
   - Provides reference on original (denormalized) scale

2. **LoRA** (`scripts/run_lora_baseline.py`)
   - Low-rank adapter on T5 attention projections
   - Supports rank, target modules, freeze policies
   - T3.B has full sweep: rank × targets × head × seeds × datasets

3. **AdaMix** (`scripts/run_adamix.py`)
   - Mixture-of-adapters with routing on HIDDEN STATES (control for "raw routing")
   - Shows routing collapse when backbone is unfrozen (demonstrates RR-MoA's innovation)
   - Supports trajectory logging for gradient analysis (T2.A)

4. **TRACE-inspired** (`scripts/run_trace_baseline.py`)
   - NOT official TRACE (no public code available)
   - Implements TRACE's core ideas:
     - Multi-scale temporal convolutions (k=3,7)
     - Gated importance weighting (simplified Gated DSIC)
     - Factorized prediction head (d_model → bottleneck → horizon)
   - Three variants: TRACE_RECONSTRUCTED_HEAD, TRACE_FULL, LORA_BEST

**NO MSFT baseline** — but TRACE-inspired implementation fills that role.

### 7. **`scripts/run_tier1_race.sh` - Experiment Batch**

Orchestrates Tier 1–3 experiments:

**T1.C:** Freeze grid on extended datasets (ETTh2, ETTm2, Electricity) × 3 seeds
**T1.B:** Uniform-router control (ensemble-vs-specialization) on main 3 datasets × 3 seeds
**T1.A:** DLinear reference on all 6 datasets × 3 seeds
**T2.A:** AdaMix trajectory logging (collapse & frozen control, seed 42)
**T2.B:** Weather Frozen Paradox reconciliation (seed 45)
**T3.A:** AAS macro-expert pool on main 3 datasets × 3 seeds
**T3.B:** LoRA sweep (rank × targets × head × seeds × datasets)

Total ~2.5 GPU-hours on A10G.

---

### **What Would Need to Change for New Baselines or Second Backbone:**

#### **Adding a New Baseline:**
1. Create `scripts/run_<basename>.py` with same data pipeline as `run_rr_moa.py`
2. Import `load_standard_data()`, `_detect_backbone_type()`, `compute_denorm_mse()` from `run_standard_evolution.py`
3. Return dict with keys: `{"mse": float, "mae": float, "mse_denorm": float (optional)}`
4. Register in `run_freeze_ablation.py` as new experiment type + call function

#### **Adding a Second Backbone (e.g., TimesFM):**

1. **In `feasibility/model.py`:**
   - Add `load_timesfm(device, model_name)` function
   - Extend `load_backbone()` with case: `elif "timesfm" in backbone_name.lower(): return load_timesfm(...)`
   - Ensure encoder blocks discoverable via `_get_encoder_blocks()`
   - Ensure hidden dim discoverable via `_get_hidden_dim()`

2. **In `scripts/run_standard_evolution.py`:**
   - Extend `_detect_backbone_type()`: `if "timesfm" in name: return "timesfm"`

3. **In `feasibility/finetune.py`:**
   - Add case to `_extract_features_batch()` for `backbone_type == "timesfm"`
   - Implement forward pass (e.g., `model(x)` or `model.encoder(x)`)

4. **For router input handling in RR-MoA:**
   - May need to adjust `_get_hidden_dim()` if TimesFM uses different config attribute
   - Raw input router is backbone-agnostic (operates on (B, input_len) time series)

5. **Testing:**
   - Run `run_rr_moa.py --backbone <timesfm-model-name> --dataset ETTh1`
   - All freeze levels, expert pools, router modes should work automatically

**Key constraint:** All backbones must expose:
- Encoder blocks via `_get_encoder_blocks()` (list of nn.Module)
- Hidden dimension via `_get_hidden_dim()` (int)
- Forward compatible with feature extraction hook or direct call

---

**File paths (absolute):**
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_rr_moa.py` — main RR-MoA experiment
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/feasibility/rrmoa_macro_experts.py` — 5 expert classes
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/feasibility/model.py` — backbone loading & discovery
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/feasibility/finetune.py` — training loop & config
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_freeze_ablation.py` — orchestration
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_tier1_race.sh` — tier 1–3 batch
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/feasibility/config.py` — adapter configuration space
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_standard_evolution.py` — data loading, backbone detection
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_trace_baseline.py` — TRACE-inspired baseline
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_lora_baseline.py` — LoRA baseline

Perfect! Now let me create one final comprehensive reference document with direct code snippets:

## Summary

I've completed a thorough exploration of the NeurIPS26 codebase infrastructure. Here's the comprehensive report:

### **1. DATA LOADING & PREPROCESSING** (`feasibility/data.py`)

**Supported Datasets:**
- 7 forecasting: ETTh1/h2/m1/m2 (remote), Weather/Electricity/Traffic (local CSV)
- 3 classification: EthanolConcentration, JapaneseVowels, BasicMotions (aeon library)

**Pipeline:**
- Sliding windows (stride=64, seq_len=512 for MOMENT)
- StandardScaler normalization: `samples = scaler.fit_transform(samples.T).T`
- Multi-horizon support: samples shape (N, input_len + forecast_horizon)

**Split Protocol** (`run_standard_evolution.py`):
- Scaler fitted **only on train data** (correct leakage prevention)
- Channel indices (`train_ch`, `val_ch`, `test_ch`) preserved for per-sample denormalization
- Default splits: ETTh1 → (8640, 2880, 2880), ETTm1 → (34560, 11520, 11520)

### **2. MSE EVALUATION**

**Normalized Space:**
```python
mse = nn.MSELoss()(preds, tgts).item()  # StandardScaler space
mae = nn.L1Loss()(preds, tgts).item()
```

**Denormalization Infrastructure** (`compute_denorm_mse`, line 121-151):
```python
# Inverse transform per-sample: denorm = normalized * scale[ch] + mean[ch]
preds_d = preds * scaler.scale_[ch_idx][:, None] + scaler.mean_[ch_idx][:, None]
mse_denorm = np.mean((preds_d - tgts_d) ** 2)
```
Returns MSE in original measurement units (ready for paper-friendly reporting).

### **3. STATISTICAL TESTING**

**Current State:**
- ❌ No formal hypothesis testing (no scipy.stats.ttest_rel)
- LoRA sweep: manual aggregation (mean/std) across seeds
- Ensemble AAS: simple delta % reporting
- `statistics.py` computes gradient/activation stats (for proxy search, not comparison)

**Reusable pattern** (from `run_lora_sweep.py` lines 130-138):
```python
mses = [e[1] for e in entries]
mean = sum(mses) / len(mses)
std = (sum((m - mean) ** 2 for m in mses) / len(mses)) ** 0.5
```

### **4. ENSEMBLE MECHANISMS**

**Existing infrastructure:**
- **`run_ensemble_aas.py`**: Average predictions from top-K adapters
  ```python
  ensemble_pred = torch.stack(all_preds).mean(dim=0)  # (B, H)
  ensemble_mse = nn.MSELoss()(ensemble_pred, Y_test_tensor).item()
  ```
- **LoRA sweep**: 3×2×2×3 = 36 configs per dataset, aggregated by best mean MSE

### **5. MOIRAI & TIMESFM**

**Moirai:** ✅ Fully integrated
- Loader: `load_moirai()` in `feasibility/model.py` line 39
- Feature extraction: patch projection in `feasibility/finetune.py` line 160
- Backbone detection: "moirai" string check in `run_standard_evolution.py` line 158

**TimesFM:** ❌ Not implemented (can be added following Moirai pattern)

### **6. EXPERIMENT ORCHESTRATION**

**Standard Evolution** (`run_standard_evolution.py`):
1. Load model → Load data (train/val/test) → Unfreeze last 4 blocks
2. LLM-guided evolution: 12 gens, pop=20, fitness=VAL MSE (3 epochs)
3. Evaluate top-5 on TEST (15 epochs)
4. Output: JSON with evolved codes + denormalized MSE

**LoRA Sweep** (`run_lora_sweep.py`):
- 36 runs per dataset, idempotent (skips existing)
- `--summarize` flag aggregates → best-per-dataset table

**Ensemble AAS** (`run_ensemble_aas.py`):
- Loads top-K codes from evolution results
- Trains each, averages predictions, compares to baseline

### **7. REUSABLE INFRASTRUCTURE FOR NEW EXPERIMENTS**

✅ **Ready to reuse:**
1. `load_standard_data()` — train/val/test splits with denorm metadata
2. `compute_denorm_mse()` — per-channel denormalization
3. `train_adapter()` — flexible training loop (3 or 15 epochs)
4. `load_backbone()` — unified MOMENT/Chronos/Moirai interface
5. `_extract_features_batch()` — backbone-agnostic feature extraction
6. Multi-seed aggregation pattern (mean/std)
7. Ensemble averaging (torch.stack().mean(dim=0))
8. JSON serialization format

❌ **Infrastructure gaps:**
- Statistical hypothesis testing (would need scipy.stats)
- Weighted ensemble (currently uniform averaging)
- TimesFM support (template exists)
- Cross-validation (only 3-fold hardcoded)

### **KEY FILE LOCATIONS**

| Purpose | File | Line |
|---------|------|------|
| Data loading | `feasibility/data.py` | - |
| Data splits | `scripts/run_standard_evolution.py` | 53-118 |
| Denorm MSE | `scripts/run_standard_evolution.py` | 121-151 |
| Train adapter | `scripts/run_standard_evolution.py` | 162-223 |
| Model loading | `feasibility/model.py` | 48 |
| LoRA attach | `feasibility/model.py` | 131 |
| Feature extraction | `feasibility/finetune.py` | 102 |
| Head factories | `feasibility/finetune.py` | 44 |
| Adapter configs | `feasibility/config.py` | 28 |
| LoRA baseline | `scripts/run_lora_baseline.py` | - |
| LoRA sweep | `scripts/run_lora_sweep.py` | - |
| Ensemble AAS | `scripts/run_ensemble_aas.py` | - |
```

     1→"""TRACE-style baseline for AAS comparison.
     2→
     3→Implements the two key ideas from TRACE (Li & Zhu, 2025):
     4→1. Importance-based LoRA module selection (Gated DSIC simplified)
     5→2. Reconstructed prediction head (factorized for parameter efficiency)
     6→
     7→This is NOT an official TRACE implementation (no public code available).
     8→We implement the core ideas to create a strong, hand-designed PEFT baseline.
     9→
    10→Usage:
    11→    python scripts/run_trace_baseline.py --dataset ETTh1
    12→    python scripts/run_trace_baseline.py --dataset ETTh1 --horizon 336
    13→"""
    14→
    15→import argparse
    16→import json
    17→import os
    18→import sys
    19→import time
    20→
    21→sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))))
    22→
    23→import numpy as np
    24→import torch
    25→import torch.nn as nn
    26→from torch.utils.data import DataLoader, TensorDataset
    27→
    28→from feasibility.model import (
    29→    load_backbone, _get_encoder_blocks, _get_hidden_dim,
    30→    _disable_gradient_checkpointing,
    31→)
    32→from feasibility.finetune import _extract_features_batch
    33→from feasibility.code_evolution import validate_adapter_code, SEED_ADAPTERS
    34→from scripts.run_standard_evolution import (
    35→    load_standard_data, train_adapter, _detect_backbone_type,
    36→)
    37→
    38→
    39→# --- TRACE-inspired adapter architectures ---
    40→
    41→TRACE_RECONSTRUCTED_HEAD = '''
    42→import torch
    43→import torch.nn as nn
    44→import torch.nn.functional as F
    45→
    46→class Adapter(nn.Module):
    47→    """TRACE-style reconstructed prediction head.
    48→
    49→    Factorizes the large (d_model -> H) projection into:
    50→    1. Pool temporal dim via learned attention weights
    51→    2. Down-project: d_model -> bottleneck (64)
    52→    3. Up-project: bottleneck -> H
    53→
    54→    This reduces params from d_model*H to d_model*64 + 64*H.
    55→    Inspired by TRACE (Li & Zhu, 2025) reconstructed heads.
    56→    """
    57→    def __init__(self, d_model, output_dim):
    58→        super().__init__()
    59→        bottleneck = 64
    60→        # Learned temporal attention (like TRACE importance weighting)
    61→        self.attn = nn.Linear(d_model, 1)
    62→        # Factorized projection
    63→        self.down = nn.Linear(d_model, bottleneck)
    64→        self.up = nn.Linear(bottleneck, output_dim)
    65→        self.norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_model)
    66→        self.act = nn.GELU()
    67→        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(0.1)
    68→
    69→    def forward(self, hidden_states):
    70→        # hidden_states: (B, T, d_model)
    71→        h = self.norm(hidden_states)
    72→        # Learned temporal attention pooling
    73→        w = torch.softmax(self.attn(h), dim=1)  # (B, T, 1)
    74→        pooled = (h * w).sum(dim=1)  # (B, d_model)
    75→        # Factorized head
    76→        out = self.down(pooled)
    77→        out = self.act(out)
    78→        out = self.dropout(out)
    79→        out = self.up(out)
    80→        return out

     1→"""Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA).
     2→
     3→Fixes the AdaMix/MoE failure: standard routers collapse because the TSFM
     4→normalization cascade (RevIN + LayerNorm) destroys hidden-state heterogeneity.
     5→RR-MoA routes on the RAW input instead, preserving the spectral and
     6→statistical diversity needed for per-sample adapter selection.
     7→
     8→Key insight: the backbone processes deep semantics, while a lightweight
     9→router reads the raw signal to select the adapter topology per-sample.
    10→
    11→Usage:
    12→    python scripts/run_rr_moa.py --dataset ETTh1
    13→    python scripts/run_rr_moa.py --dataset ETTh1 --top-k 2 --unfreeze frozen
    14→"""
    15→
    16→import argparse
    17→import json
    18→import os
    19→import sys
    20→import time
    21→
    22→sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))))

    25→import torch
    26→import torch.nn as nn
    27→import torch.nn.functional as F

    31→    load_backbone, _get_encoder_blocks, _get_hidden_dim,
    32→    _disable_gradient_checkpointing,

    41→    MACRO_EXPERT_CLASSES, MACRO_EXPERT_NAMES,
    42→)

    44→
    45→# --- Expert adapter heads (same as AdaMix) ---
    46→
    47→class MeanPoolHead(nn.Module):
    48→    def __init__(self, d_model, output_dim, hidden=64):
    49→        super().__init__()
    50→        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(d_model, hidden), nn.GELU(), nn.Linear(hidden, output_dim))
    51→    def forward(self, h):
    52→        return self.net(h.mean(dim=1))

    57→        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(d_model, hidden), nn.GELU(), nn.Linear(hidden, output_dim))
    58→    def forward(self, h):
    59→        return self.net(h[:, -1, :])

     1→"""Ensemble AAS: average predictions from top-K discovered adapters.
     2→
     3→Instead of picking the single best adapter, uses all top-K adapters
     4→discovered by AAS and averages their predictions at test time.
     5→Zero additional search cost — the K adapters are already discovered.
     6→
     7→Usage:
     8→    python scripts/run_ensemble_aas.py --dataset ETTh1
     9→    python scripts/run_ensemble_aas.py --dataset ETTh1 --top-k 3
    10→"""
    11→
    12→import argparse
    13→import json
    14→import os
    15→import sys
    16→import time
    17→
    18→sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))))
    19→

    21→import torch
    22→import torch.nn as nn

    24→
    25→from feasibility.model import (
